It is worth notin<sub>g</sub> that criteria can be mutuall<sub>y</sub> exclusive in real world scenarios. Hence it is crucial for <sub>p</sub>ractitioners to select the correct metrics<sub>,</sub> which is <sub>p</sub>roblem-de<sub>p</sub>endent.

Kleinberg et al. and Chouldechova obtained analogous results for calibration and error-rate balance [12, 31]. The famous COMPAS risk assessment tool is a <sub>p</sub>rominent exam<sub>p</sub>le: accordin<sub>g</sub> to a ProPublica investi<sub>g</sub>ation [32], Black defendants who did not reofend were classified as hi<sub>g</sub>her risk <sub>a</sub>t <sub>near</sub>l<sub>y</sub> t<sub>w</sub>i<sub>ce</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>ra</sub>t<sub>e</sub> <sub>o</sub>f <sub>w</sub>hit<sub>e</sub> d<sub>e</sub>f<sub>en</sub>d<sub>an</sub>t<sub>s.</sub> Th<sub>e</sub> d<sub>eve</sub>l<sub>oper</sub> <sub>rep</sub>li<sub>e</sub>d th<sub>a</sub>t th<sub>e</sub> <sub>scores</sub> <sub>were</sub> <sub>equa</sub>ll<sub>y</sub> <sub>p</sub>redictive across <sub>g</sub>rou<sub>p</sub>s [4, 13]. Single Sensitive Feature Logic and Intersectionality. Real-world audits often require evaluating indi<sub>v</sub>id<sub>u</sub>als at the intersection of m<sub>u</sub>lti<sub>p</sub>le sensiti<sub>v</sub>e attrib<sub>u</sub>tes. Gi<sub>v</sub>en a <sub>v</sub>ector of attrib<sub>u</sub>tes $A _ { v e c } =$ $\left[ A _ { 1 } , A _ { 2 } , . . . , A _ { n } \right]$ <sub>,</sub> we com<sub>p</sub>ute the Cartesian <sub>p</sub>roduct of their <sub>p</sub>ossible assi<sub>g</sub>nments to construct a <sub>un</sub>ifi<sub>e</sub>d <sub>ca</sub>t<sub>egor</sub>i<sub>ca</sub>l <sub>space</sub> $\mathcal { A } _ { c o m b }$ <sub>.</sub> Th<sub>a</sub>t i<sub>s,</sub> <sub>we</sub> m<sub>ap</sub> <sub>a</sub>n $( N _ { \mathrm { e n t i t i e s } } \times N _ { \mathrm { f e a t u r e s } } )$ <sub>a</sub>ttrib<sub>u</sub>t<sub>e</sub> m<sub>a</sub>trix int<sub>o</sub> <sub>a</sub> vector where each uni<sub>q</sub>ue intersectional <sub>p</sub>rofile is ma<sub>pp</sub>ed to a sin<sub>g</sub>le distinct inte<sub>g</sub>er.

Multi-Dimensional Disparity. When the outcome space is artificially simplified via naive binarization or one-vs-rest reduction a<sub>pp</sub>roaches<sub>,</sub> fairness constraints on a sin<sub>g</sub>le tar<sub>g</sub>et fail to account for how misclassifications are <sub>p</sub>artitioned across the remainin<sub>g</sub> cate<sub>g</sub>ories. We can refer to this blind spot as Multi-Dimensional Disparity. To address Multi-Dimensional Disparity, we adopt a Max-Dif A<sub>gg</sub>re<sub>g</sub>ation a<sub>pp</sub>roach [39]. ## 3.2 Rebuilding from the Criteria: Independence

The first group fairness criterion is Independence, which requires that the predictive outcome $\hat { Y }$ operates completely independently of the sensitive attribute �, i.e. $\hat { Y }$ ⊥⊥ �. This criterion i<sub>g</sub>nores th<sub>e</sub> <sub>groun</sub>d t<sub>ru</sub>th $Y ,$ <sub>w</sub>hi<sub>c</sub>h <sub>ma</sub>k<sub>es</sub> it <sub>su</sub>it<sub>a</sub>bl<sub>e</sub> i<sub>n</sub> <sub>scenar</sub>i<sub>os</sub> <sub>w</sub>h<sub>ere</sub> th<sub>e</sub> t<sub>rue</sub> l<sub>a</sub>b<sub>e</sub>l i<sub>s</sub> <sub>unava</sub>il<sub>a</sub>bl<sub>e</sub> <sub>or</sub> cannot be reliably trusted. Unless the <sub>g</sub>round truth is <sub>p</sub>erfectl<sub>y</sub> inde<sub>p</sub>endent of the sensitive <sub>a</sub>tt<sub>r</sub>ib<sub>u</sub>t<sub>e,</sub> <sub>w</sub>hi<sub>c</sub>h <sub>rare</sub>l<sub>y</sub> h<sub>o</sub>ld<sub>s</sub> i<sub>n</sub> <sub>prac</sub>ti<sub>ce,</sub> <sub>a</sub> “<sub>per</sub>f<sub>ec</sub>t <sub>c</sub>l<sub>ass</sub>ifi<sub>er</sub>” <sub>–</sub> <sub>a</sub> <sub>c</sub>l<sub>ass</sub>ifi<sub>er</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t <sub>comm</sub>it<sub>s</sub> <sub>no</sub> <sub>error</sub> <sub>–</sub> generally does not satisfy Independence.

This metric demands e<sub>q</sub>ual selection rates across <sub>p</sub>rotected <sub>g</sub>rou<sub>p</sub>s for each tar<sub>g</sub>et.

In the case of binar<sub>y</sub> classification [11, 34, 37, 40], DP is defined as:

In the binar<sub>y</sub> settin<sub>g,</sub> demandin<sub>g</sub> e<sub>q</sub>ual PPR across <sub>p</sub>rotected <sub>g</sub>rou<sub>p</sub>s im<sub>p</sub>lies e<sub>q</sub>ual Ne<sub>g</sub>- ative Prediction Rate (NPR). Conditional Demo<sub>g</sub>ra<sub>p</sub>hic Parit<sub>y</sub> (CDP), also known as Conditional Statistical Parity, is an extension of the Independence criterion that requires model’s <sub>p</sub>redictions $\hat { Y }$ t<sub>o</sub> b<sub>e</sub> i<sub>n</sub>d<sub>epen</sub>d<sub>en</sub>t <sub>o</sub>f th<sub>e</sub> <sub>sens</sub>iti<sub>ve</sub> <sub>a</sub>tt<sub>r</sub>ib<sub>u</sub>t<sub>e</sub> �<sub>,</sub> <sub>g</sub>i<sub>ven</sub> <sub>a</sub> <sub>spec</sub>ifi<sub>c</sub> <sub>se</sub>t <sub>o</sub>f <sub>con</sub>t<sub>ro</sub>l <sub>var</sub>i<sub>a</sub>bl<sub>es</sub> k<sub>nown</sub> <sub>as</sub> “<sub>s</sub>t<sub>ra</sub>t<sub>a</sub>” �<sub>:</sub> $\bar { Y }$ ⊥⊥ � | �. ## 3.3 Rebuilding from the Criteria: Separation

The Separation criterion requires the prediction $\hat { Y }$ <sub>an</sub>d th<sub>e sens</sub>iti<sub>ve a</sub>tt<sub>r</sub>ib<sub>u</sub>t<sub>e</sub> � t<sub>o</sub> b<sub>e con</sub>diti<sub>ona</sub>ll<sub>y</sub> independent given the true label �, i.e. $\hat { Y }$ ⊥⊥ � | � . Unlike Independence, Separation requires information about the tar<sub>g</sub>et �<sub>,</sub> which means it could <sub>p</sub>otentiall<sub>y</sub> <sub>p</sub>er<sub>p</sub>etuate <sub>p</sub>re-existin<sub>g</sub> bias present in the ground truth �. Moreover, Separation is a “conservative” criterion, meaning that a “<sub>per</sub>f<sub>ec</sub>t <sub>c</sub>l<sub>ass</sub>ifi<sub>er</sub>” $( { \hat { Y } } = Y )$ <sub>a</sub>l<sub>ways sa</sub>ti<sub>s</sub>fi<sub>es</sub> th<sub>e cr</sub>it<sub>er</sub>i<sub>on.</sub>

3.3.1 E<sub>q</sub>ualized Odds. EO (E<sub>q</sub>ualized Odds) is the <sub>g</sub>rou<sub>p</sub>-fairness metrics that evaluates com<sub>p</sub>liance with Se<sub>p</sub>aration for a ML model’s <sub>p</sub>redictions. E<sub>q</sub>ualized Odds is satisfied when the confusion matrix looks statistically similar for every sensitive group. Indeed, it is commonly defined as the error-parity metric [11]. It is worth noting that this requirement is strictly stronger than simply imposing equal <sub>overa</sub>ll <sub>accuracy</sub> <sub>across</sub> <sub>pro</sub>t<sub>ec</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>groups.</sub> Whil<sub>e</sub> EO <sub>en</sub>f<sub>orces</sub> <sub>equa</sub>l <sub>ra</sub>t<sub>es</sub> f<sub>or</sub> <sub>eac</sub>h <sub>spec</sub>ifi<sub>c</sub> <sub>error</sub> t<sub>yp</sub>e amon<sub>g</sub> <sub>g</sub>rou<sub>p</sub>s<sub>,</sub> e<sub>q</sub>ual overall accurac<sub>y</sub> a<sub>gg</sub>re<sub>g</sub>ates them checkin<sub>g</sub> for e<sub>q</sub>ual unconditioned mi<sub>sc</sub>l<sub>ass</sub>ifi<sub>ca</sub>ti<sub>o</sub>n r<sub>a</sub>t<sub>es.</sub> T<sub>o</sub> ill<sub>us</sub>tr<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e</sub> thi<sub>s</sub> di<sub>s</sub>tin<sub>c</sub>ti<sub>o</sub>n <sub>we</sub> <sub>p</sub>r<sub>ov</sub>id<sub>e</sub> Ex<sub>a</sub>m<sub>p</sub>l<sub>e</sub> 6 in A<sub>ppe</sub>ndix A<sub>.</sub> Alt<sub>e</sub>rn<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>ve</sub> f<sub>a</sub>i<sub>rness me</sub>t<sub>r</sub>i<sub>cs</sub> f<sub>ocus</sub>i<sub>ng so</sub>l<sub>e</sub>l<sub>y on equa</sub>l <sub>accuracy</sub> h<sub>ave</sub> b<sub>een propose</sub>d i<sub>n</sub> th<sub>e</sub> lit<sub>era</sub>t<sub>ure;</sub> th<sub>ey w</sub>ill b<sub>e</sub> d<sub>escr</sub>ib<sub>e</sub>d i<sub>n</sub> d<sub>e</sub>t<sub>a</sub>il i<sub>n</sub> S<sub>ec</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> 3<sub>.</sub>5<sub>.</sub>

For a binar<sub>y</sub> outcome, E<sub>q</sub>ualized Odds [11, 34, 37, 40] re<sub>q</sub>uires both the True Positive Rate (TPR) and False Positive Rate (FPR) to be equal across all protected groups, i.e.:

$$
P ( \hat { Y } = 1 \mid A = a , Y = y ) = P ( \hat { Y } = 1 \mid A = b , Y = y ) \quad \forall a , b \in \mathcal { A } , \forall y \in \{ 0 , 1 \} .\tag{6}
$$

The literat<sub>u</sub>re <sub>p</sub>ro<sub>v</sub>ides t<sub>w</sub>o relaxations for the binar<sub>y</sub> metrics:

• Predictive E<sub>q</sub>ualit<sub>y</sub>, which focuses on FPR (� = 0) and <sub>g</sub>uarantees e<sub>q</sub>ual True Ne<sub>g</sub>ative Rate (TNR) as a b<sub>yp</sub>roduct.

As observed by Hardt et. al [28], the general definition of the Separation criterion already account f<sub>or</sub> b<sub>o</sub>th bi<sub>nary</sub> <sub>or</sub> <sub>mu</sub>lti<sub>-c</sub>l<sub>ass</sub> t<sub>arge</sub>t<sub>s</sub> <sub>an</sub>d <sub>sens</sub>iti<sub>ve</sub> f<sub>ea</sub>t<sub>ures.</sub> C<sub>onsequen</sub>tl<sub>y,</sub> i<sub>n</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>mu</sub>lti<sub>-c</sub>l<sub>ass</sub> <sub>an</sub>d <sub>mu</sub>lti<sub>-group</sub> <sub>scenar</sub>i<sub>o,</sub> EO i<sub>s</sub> d<sub>e</sub>fi<sub>ne</sub>d <sub>as</sub> f<sub>o</sub>ll<sub>ows:</sub>

• Of-dia<sub>g</sub>onal E<sub>q</sub>ualit<sub>y</sub>, which focuses on e<sub>q</sub>ual of-dia<sub>g</sub>onal misclassification rates $( i . e . \hat { Y } \neq Y )$ <sup>across</sup> g<sup>rou</sup>p<sup>s.</sup>

3.3.2 Relaxed Se<sub>p</sub>aration Criteria. EO mandates strict conditions to be satisfied<sub>;</sub> th<sub>u</sub>s<sub>,</sub> scholars d<sub>eve</sub>l<sub>o e</sub>d <sub>re</sub>l<sub>axa</sub>ti<sub>ons</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t <sub>are eas</sub>i<sub>er</sub> t<sub>o sa</sub>ti<sub>s</sub>f <sub>.</sub> I<sub>n</sub> th<sub>e</sub> bi<sub>nar se</sub>tti<sub>n ,</sub> E <sub>ua</sub>li<sub>ze</sub>d Odd<sub>s re</sub>l<sub>axa</sub>ti<sub>ons</sub> restrict e<sub>q</sub>ual <sub>p</sub>robabilit<sub>y</sub> constraints to individual confusion matrix columns � overt the confusion matrices constructed for each <sub>g</sub>rou<sub>p</sub>s. Extendin<sub>g</sub> the <sub>p</sub>robabilistic metric definitions to the multi class domain breaks this column-wise ali<sub>g</sub>nment. Conse<sub>q</sub>uentl<sub>y,</sub> Dia<sub>g</sub>onal E<sub>q</sub>ualit<sub>y</sub> isolates the main dia<sub>g</sub>onal entries $( n _ { k k } )$ <sub>,</sub> conversel<sub>y,</sub> Of-dia<sub>g</sub>onal E<sub>q</sub>ualit<sub>y</sub> constrains all of-dia<sub>g</sub>onal entries $( n _ { k j }$ <sub>w</sub>h<sub>ere</sub> $k \neq j )$ <sub>.</sub> T<sub>oge</sub>th<sub>er,</sub> th<sub>ese</sub> t<sub>wo</sub> <sub>mu</sub>lti<sub>-c</sub>l<sub>ass</sub> <sub>re</sub>l<sub>axa</sub>ti<sub>ons</sub> <sub>par</sub>titi<sub>on</sub> th<sub>e</sub> f<sub>u</sub>ll <sub>con</sub>f<sub>us</sub>i<sub>on</sub> <sub>ma</sub>t<sub>r</sub>i<sub>x,</sub> <sub>suc</sub>h th<sub>a</sub>t <sub>sa</sub>ti<sub>s</sub>f<sub>y</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> b<sub>o</sub>th <sub>s</sub>i<sub>mu</sub>lt<sub>aneous</sub>l<sub>y</sub> i<sub>s ma</sub>th<sub>ema</sub>ti<sub>ca</sub>ll<sub>y equ</sub>i<sub>va</sub>l<sub>en</sub>t t<sub>o ac</sub>hi<sub>ev</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> f<sub>u</sub>ll <sub>mu</sub>lti<sub>-c</sub>l<sub>ass</sub> Se<sub>p</sub>aration.

E<sub>q</sub>ual O<sub>pp</sub>ortunit<sub>y</sub> is the first binar<sub>y</sub> relaxation <sub>p</sub>ro<sub>p</sub>osed in the literature<sub>,</sub> which enforces e<sub>q</sub>ual TPR across <sub>p</sub>rotected <sub>g</sub>rou<sub>p</sub>s [11, 34, 37, 40]:

$$
\begin{array} { r } { P ( \hat { Y } = 1 \mid A = a , Y = 1 ) = P ( \hat { Y } = 1 \mid A = b , Y = 1 ) \quad \forall a , b \in \mathcal { A } . } \end{array}\tag{8}
$$

In the binar<sub>y</sub> settin<sub>g,</sub> enforcin<sub>g</sub> E<sub>q</sub>ual O<sub>pp</sub>ortunit<sub>y</sub> across <sub>p</sub>rotected <sub>g</sub>rou<sub>p</sub>s inherentl<sub>y</sub> <sub>y</sub>ields e<sub>q</sub>ual complementary rates (i.e., FNR).

Predictive E<sub>q</sub>ualit<sub>y</sub> com<sub>p</sub>lements E<sub>q</sub>ual O<sub>pp</sub>ortunit<sub>y</sub> b<sub>y</sub> enforcin<sub>g</sub> e<sub>q</sub>ual FPR across <sub>p</sub>rotected <sub>g</sub>rou<sub>p</sub>s [11]:

$$
\begin{array} { r } { P ( \hat { Y } = 1 \mid A = a , Y = 0 ) = P ( \hat { Y } = 1 \mid A = b , Y = 0 ) \quad \forall a , b \in \mathcal { A } . } \end{array}\tag{9}
$$

In this formulation<sub>,</sub> enforcin<sub>g</sub> Predictive E<sub>q</sub>ualit<sub>y</sub> across <sub>p</sub>rotected <sub>g</sub>rou<sub>p</sub>s inherentl<sub>y</sub> re<sub>q</sub>uires e<sub>q</sub>ual complementary rates (i.e., TNR).

Existin<sub>g</sub> literature uses inconsistent terms when referrin<sub>g</sub> to multi-class E<sub>q</sub>ualized Odds relaxation, such as multi-class Equal Opportunity, Overall Accuracy, Overall Misclassification Rate or Max Loss Discrepancy. To resolve this ambiguity, we propose the taxonomy Diagonal Equality and Of-diagonal Equality, whose definitions are given below.

Dia<sub>g</sub>onal E<sub>q</sub>ualit<sub>y</sub> is the first multi-class relaxation<sub>,</sub> and it re<sub>q</sub>uires the <sub>p</sub>redictor to score e<sub>q</sub>ual <sub>reca</sub>ll <sub>across</sub> <sub>every</sub> <sub>pro</sub>t<sub>ec</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>group.</sub> I<sub>n</sub> <sub>o</sub>th<sub>er</sub> <sub>wor</sub>d<sub>s,</sub> it <sub>requ</sub>i<sub>res</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>con</sub>f<sub>us</sub>i<sub>on</sub> <sub>ma</sub>t<sub>r</sub>i<sub>ces</sub> <sub>o</sub>f <sub>a</sub>ll protected groups to have identical diagonals. Earlier work by Rouzot et al. presented a multi-class generalization named Equal Opportunity, also known as Overall Accuracy [43]. Although similar to our notion<sub>,</sub> the authors limited their definition of the metric to a sin<sub>g</sub>le binar<sub>y</sub> sensitive attribute. Conversely, Purificato et al. [38], proposed the following complete multi-class and multi-group f<sub>ormu</sub>l<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>on:</sub>

$$
\begin{array} { r } { P ( \hat { Y } = k | A = a , Y = k ) = P ( \hat { Y } = k | A = b , Y = k ) , \quad \forall k \in \mathcal { Y } , \forall a , b \in \mathcal { R } . } \end{array}\tag{10}
$$

Note that in their work, Purificato et al. state that Equation 10 would also apply as the extension of EO in the multi-class and multi-group setting. In contrast, given Hardt et al. definition of Equal O<sub>pp</sub>ortunit<sub>y</sub> [28] (see E<sub>q</sub>uation 7), it is clear that the two <sub>p</sub>robabilistic formulations difer, where E<sub>q</sub>uation 10 does not full<sub>y</sub> enforce Se<sub>p</sub>aration Criterion across the whole confusion matrix.

Finall<sub>y,</sub> Of-dia<sub>g</sub>onal E<sub>q</sub>ualit<sub>y</sub> mandates e<sub>q</sub>ual misclassification rates across <sub>p</sub>rotected <sub>g</sub>rou<sub>p</sub>s. Rouzot et al. described a multi-class generalization named Overall Misclassification Rate, also known as Max Loss Discrepancy [43], defined as the complementary probability of Diagonal Equality:

$$
\begin{array} { r } { P ( \hat { Y } \neq j \mid A = a , Y = j ) = P ( \hat { Y } \neq j \mid A = b , Y = j ) , \quad \forall j \in y ; \forall a , b \in \mathcal { A } . } \end{array}\tag{11}
$$

Analo<sub>g</sub>ousl<sub>y,</sub> the<sub>y</sub> limited their definition of the metric to a sin<sub>g</sub>le binar<sub>y</sub> sensitive attribute. This definition sufers from aggregation, which is a critical limitation similar to the EO vs Equal Accuracy <sub>scenar</sub>i<sub>o,</sub> <sub>w</sub>h<sub>ere</sub> E<sub>qua</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> 11 <sub>on</sub>l<sub>y</sub> <sub>c</sub>h<sub>ec</sub>k<sub>s</sub> f<sub>or</sub> <sub>overa</sub>ll <sub>m</sub>i<sub>sc</sub>l<sub>ass</sub>ifi<sub>ca</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> <sub>ra</sub>t<sub>es,</sub> l<sub>oos</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> i<sub>n</sub>f<sub>orma</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> re<sub>g</sub>ardin<sub>g</sub> <sub>p</sub>otential s<sub>y</sub>stematic dis<sub>p</sub>arities. We tackle this <sub>g</sub>a<sub>p</sub> in the literature b<sub>y</sub> <sub>p</sub>rovidin<sub>g</sub> the followin<sub>g</sub> multi-<sub>g</sub>rou<sub>p</sub> and multi-class <sub>g</sub>eneralization named Of-dia<sub>g</sub>onal E<sub>q</sub>ualit<sub>y</sub>:

$$
P ( \hat { Y } = k \mid A = a , Y = j ) = P ( \hat { Y } = k \mid A = b , Y = j ) , \quad \forall k , j \in \mathcal { Y } ; j \neq k , \forall a , b \in \mathcal { A } .\tag{12}
$$

Proposition 1 (Equivalence to Multi-Class Equalized Odds). Satisfying Eq. 12 is mathematically equivalent to satisfying full multi-class Equalized Odds (parity across the entire conditional confusion matrix). (See Proof 1 in Appendix B.)

We stress that this im<sub>p</sub>lication onl<sub>y</sub> holds when Of-dia<sub>g</sub>onal E<sub>q</sub>ualit<sub>y</sub> is strictl<sub>y</sub> satisfied across all tar<sub>g</sub>ets and <sub>p</sub>rotected <sub>g</sub>rou<sub>p</sub>s<sub>,</sub> a condition rarel<sub>y</sub> met in real world scenarios. Hence<sub>,</sub> introducin<sub>g</sub> this relaxation remains hi<sub>g</sub>hl<sub>y</sub> relevant as it evaluates <sub>p</sub>urel<sub>y</sub> of-dia<sub>g</sub>onal dis<sub>p</sub>arities.

Suficiency is the third group fairness criterion defined by Barocas et al. [5]. Exam<sub>p</sub>le 11 in A<sub>pp</sub>endix A hi<sub>g</sub>hli<sub>g</sub>hts the diference between Se<sub>p</sub>aration and Suficienc<sub>y</sub>. Ultimatel<sub>y,</sub> Suficienc<sub>y</sub> is <sub>p</sub>articularl<sub>y</sub> desirable in a<sub>pp</sub>lications where the reliabilit<sub>y</sub> of <sub>p</sub>redictions <sub>s</sub>h<sub>ou</sub>ld <sub>no</sub>t d<sub>epen</sub>d <sub>on</sub> <sub>sens</sub>iti<sub>ve</sub> <sub>a</sub>tt<sub>r</sub>ib<sub>u</sub>t<sub>es,</sub> <sub>suc</sub>h <sub>as</sub> <sub>c</sub>li<sub>n</sub>i<sub>ca</sub>l d<sub>ec</sub>i<sub>s</sub>i<sub>on</sub> <sub>suppor</sub>t <sub>an</sub>d <sub>r</sub>i<sub>s</sub>k <sub>assessmen</sub>t <sup>s</sup>y<sup>stems.</sup>

To the best of our knowled<sub>g</sub>e<sub>,</sub> scientific literature onl<sub>y</sub> covers Suficienc<sub>y</sub> in binar<sub>y</sub> scenarios. In this work<sub>,</sub> we address this <sub>g</sub>a<sub>p</sub> b<sub>y</sub> <sub>p</sub>ro<sub>p</sub>osin<sub>g</sub> a multi-class and multi-<sub>g</sub>rou<sub>p</sub> metric extension for P<sub>re</sub>di<sub>c</sub>ti<sub>ve</sub> P<sub>ar</sub>it<sub>y</sub> <sub>as</sub> f<sub>orma</sub>li<sub>ze</sub>d <sub>as</sub> f<sub>o</sub>ll<sub>ow:</sub>

N<sub>o</sub>ti<sub>ce</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t <sub>ou</sub>r m<sub>u</sub>lti-<sub>c</sub>l<sub>ass</sub> f<sub>o</sub>rm<sub>u</sub>l<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>o</sub>n d<sub>oes</sub> n<sub>o</sub>t <sub>ge</sub>n<sub>e</sub>r<sub>a</sub>li<sub>se</sub> th<sub>e</sub> bin<sub>a</sub>r<sub>y</sub> d<sub>e</sub>finiti<sub>o</sub>n<sub>.</sub> A<sub>s</sub> <sub>p</sub>r<sub>ev</sub>i<sub>ous</sub>l<sub>y</sub> stated<sub>,</sub> the ori<sub>g</sub>inal Predictive Parit<sub>y</sub> restrict e<sub>q</sub>ualit<sub>y</sub> strictl<sub>y</sub> to $Y = { \hat { Y } } = 1$ assumin<sub>g</sub> a binar<sub>y</sub> scenario. $$
P ( Y = 0 \mid A = a , { \hat { Y } } = 0 ) = P ( Y = 0 \mid A = b , { \hat { Y } } = 0 )

$$
P ( Y = 1 \mid A = a , \hat { Y } = 1 ) = P ( Y = 1 \mid A = b , \hat { Y } = 1 ) , \quad \forall a , b \in \mathcal { A }\tag{15}

## 3.5 Rebuilding from the Criteria: Accuracy

Although Barocas et al. [5] proposed only three criteria (Independence, Separation, Suficiency), <sub>a</sub>l<sub>go</sub>rithmi<sub>c</sub> f<sub>a</sub>irn<sub>ess</sub> lit<sub>e</sub>r<sub>a</sub>t<sub>u</sub>r<sub>e cove</sub>r<sub>s a</sub> f<sub>ou</sub>rth <sub>co</sub>nditi<sub>o</sub>n: A<sub>ccu</sub>r<sub>acy.</sub> F<sub>o</sub>rm<sub>a</sub>ll<sub>y,</sub> A<sub>ccu</sub>r<sub>acy</sub> d<sub>oes</sub> n<sub>o</sub>t constitute a distinct criterion<sub>;</sub> mathematicall<sub>y,</sub> re<sub>q</sub>uirin<sub>g</sub> e<sub>q</sub>ual overall accurac<sub>y</sub> across <sub>p</sub>rotected <sub>groups</sub> d<sub>oes</sub> <sub>no</sub>t <sub>a</sub>li<sub>gn</sub> <sub>w</sub>ith <sub>or</sub> i<sub>mp</sub>l<sub>y</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>me</sub>t<sub>r</sub>i<sub>cs</sub> <sub>assoc</sub>i<sub>a</sub>t<sub>e</sub>d <sub>w</sub>ith th<sub>e</sub> th<sub>ree</sub> <sub>we</sub>ll<sub>-es</sub>t<sub>a</sub>bli<sub>s</sub>h<sub>e</sub>d <sub>cr</sub>it<sub>er</sub>i<sub>a.</sub>

We stress once a<sub>g</sub>ain the diference between re<sub>q</sub>uirin<sub>g</sub> e<sub>q</sub>ual overall accurac<sub>y</sub> across <sub>p</sub>rotected <sub>g</sub>rou<sub>p</sub>s and enforcin<sub>g</sub> Se<sub>p</sub>aration (or Suficienc<sub>y</sub>), where evaluatin<sub>g</sub> accurac<sub>y</sub> alone as a fairness metric can be misleadin<sub>g</sub> and ma<sub>y</sub> hide severe ML <sub>p</sub>rejudices (see Exam<sub>p</sub>le 6 in A<sub>pp</sub>endix A). S<sub>pec</sub>ifi<sub>ca</sub>ll<sub>y,</sub> A<sub>ccu</sub>r<sub>acy</sub> <sub>c</sub>rit<sub>e</sub>ri<sub>a</sub> <sub>eva</sub>l<sub>ua</sub>t<sub>es</sub> <sub>u</sub>n<sub>co</sub>nditi<sub>o</sub>n<sub>a</sub>l t<sub>o</sub>t<sub>a</sub>l <sub>co</sub>rr<sub>ec</sub>tn<sub>ess</sub> <sub>a</sub>nd r<sub>es</sub>tri<sub>c</sub>t<sub>s</sub> <sub>o</sub>nl<sub>y</sub> th<sub>e</sub> a<sub>gg</sub>re<sub>g</sub>ate <sub>p</sub>ro<sub>p</sub>ortion of decisions<sub>,</sub> <sub>p</sub>ermittin<sub>g</sub> the model to commit vastl<sub>y</sub> diferent t<sub>yp</sub>es of errors across <sub>p</sub>rotected <sub>g</sub>rou<sub>p</sub>s. Moreover<sub>,</sub> alon<sub>g</sub>side Se<sub>p</sub>aration and Suficienc<sub>y,</sub> Accurac<sub>y</sub> is considered a “<sub>conserva</sub>ti<sub>ve</sub>” <sub>cr</sub>it<sub>er</sub>i<sub>a</sub> b<sub>ecause</sub> it i<sub>s</sub> t<sub>r</sub>i<sub>v</sub>i<sub>a</sub>ll<sub>y sa</sub>ti<sub>s</sub>fi<sub>e</sub>d b<sub>y a</sub> “<sub>per</sub>f<sub>ec</sub>t” <sub>c</sub>l<sub>ass</sub>ifi<sub>er</sub> $( { \hat { Y } } = Y )$ .

3.5.1 Equal Accuracy. Equal Accuracy, also known as Accuracy Parity or Overall Accuracy Equality, is a <sub>g</sub>rou<sub>p</sub> fairness metric that<sub>,</sub> as the name su<sub>gg</sub>ests<sub>,</sub> re<sub>q</sub>uires overall <sub>p</sub>rediction accurac<sub>y</sub> across protected groups. Castelnovo et al. [11] covered the binary setting, which we consequently extend <sub>as</sub> f<sub>o</sub>ll<sub>ows:</sub>

$$
\begin{array} { r } { P ( Y = \hat { Y } \mid A = a ) = P ( Y = \hat { Y } \mid A = b ) , \quad \forall a , b \in \mathcal { A } . } \end{array}\tag{16}
$$

All results in this manuscri<sub>p</sub>t are based on FairMetrics<sup>1</sup> [27], an o<sub>p</sub>en-source P<sub>y</sub>Torch-based P<sub>y</sub>thon librar<sub>y</sub> for binar<sub>y,</sub> multi-class<sub>,</sub> and multi-<sub>g</sub>rou<sub>p</sub> fairness evaluation. The codebase com<sub>p</sub>rises two main <sub>p</sub>ackages: (i) fairmetrics/, the core evaluation engine containing discrete fairness metrics functions organized by foundational criteria; and (ii) fairmetrics\_experiments/, the experimental suite containin<sub>g</sub> data loaders<sub>,</sub> tar<sub>g</sub>et binarization strate<sub>g</sub>ies<sub>,</sub> and the H<sub>yp</sub>er<sub>p</sub>arameters O<sub>p</sub>timization (HPO) evaluation runner. The runner su<sub>pp</sub>orts native multi-class evaluation alon<sub>g</sub>side binarization <sub>re</sub>d<sub>uc</sub>ti<sub>ons</sub> t<sub>o quan</sub>tif<sub>y</sub> th<sub>e emp</sub>i<sub>r</sub>i<sub>ca</sub>l “<sub>re</sub>d<sub>uc</sub>ti<sub>on gap</sub>”<sub>, w</sub>ith <sub>a</sub>ll <sub>exper</sub>i<sub>men</sub>t<sub>s</sub> f<sub>u</sub>ll<sub>y repro</sub>d<sub>uc</sub>ibl<sub>e v</sub>i<sub>a a</sub> sin<sub>g</sub>le batch scri<sub>p</sub>t ex<sub>p</sub>ortin<sub>g</sub> structured JSON results.

In order to com<sub>p</sub>are binar<sub>y</sub> and multi-class/multi-<sub>g</sub>rou<sub>p</sub> metrics<sub>,</sub> we a<sub>pp</sub>lied FairMetrics to two <sub>open</sub> d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>ase</sub>t<sub>s</sub> f<sub>or</sub> f<sub>a</sub>i<sub>rness</sub> i<sub>n c</sub>l<sub>ass</sub>ifi<sub>ca</sub>ti<sub>on, as</sub> d<sub>escr</sub>ib<sub>e</sub>d i<sub>n</sub> th<sub>e nex</sub>t <sub>su</sub>b<sub>sec</sub>ti<sub>on.</sub>

## 5.1 Experimental Setup

We evaluated our metrics on two widel established tabular datasets in the fair ML: Adult [7] and Students’ Dropout and Academic Success [33]. Both datasets are desi ned for the classification tasks <sub>an</sub>d<sub>,</sub> i<sub>n</sub> th<sub>e</sub>i<sub>r or</sub>i<sub>g</sub>i<sub>na</sub>l f<sub>ormu</sub>l<sub>a</sub>ti<sub>ons, are c</sub>h<sub>arac</sub>t<sub>er</sub>i<sub>ze</sub>d b<sub>y c</sub>l<sub>ass</sub> i<sub>m</sub>b<sub>a</sub>l<sub>ance an</sub>d <sub>sens</sub>iti<sub>ve</sub> d<sub>emograp</sub>hi<sub>c</sub> f<sub>ea</sub>t<sub>ures.</sub> C<sub>urren</sub>tl<sub>y,</sub> th<sub>e</sub> lit<sub>era</sub>t<sub>ure prov</sub>id<sub>es</sub> f<sub>ew rea</sub>l<sub>-wor</sub>ld <sub>mu</sub>lti<sub>-c</sub>l<sub>ass</sub> d<sub>a</sub>t<sub>ase</sub>t<sub>s</sub> f<sub>or</sub> f<sub>a</sub>i<sub>rness eva</sub>l<sub>ua</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> and fewer studies re<sub>g</sub>ardin<sub>g</sub> multi-class fairness a<sub>pp</sub>roaches to ali<sub>g</sub>n with. To maintain realistic feature distributions without resortin<sub>g</sub> to s<sub>y</sub>nthetic <sub>g</sub>eneration<sub>,</sub> we created a custom multi-class target for the Adult dataset by categorizing individuals into three classes based on income and ed<sub>u</sub>cation level. See Ex<sub>p</sub>lorator<sub>y</sub> Data Anal<sub>y</sub>sis in A<sub>pp</sub>endix C. All ex<sub>p</sub>eriments were exec<sub>u</sub>ted on a virtualized Ubuntu 24.04 LTS 23-node cluster (Intel Xeon Gold / AMD EPYC CPUs, u<sub>p</sub> to 400 GB RAM) and a dedicated Virtual Machine (VM) (12 vCPUs AMD EPYC, 94 GiB RAM) backed b<sub>y</sub> Network Attached Stora<sub>g</sub>e (NAS). Throu<sub>g</sub>hout our ex<sub>p</sub>eriments, in order to ensure re<sub>p</sub>roducibilit<sub>y</sub> we set random seeds to 42 for the initial 10-fold stratified s<sub>p</sub>lit (StratifiedKFold) <sub>an</sub>d th<sub>e</sub> f<sub>o</sub>ld i<sub>n</sub>d<sub>ex</sub> $k \in \{ 0 , . . . , 9 \}$ for fold-level Random Forest (RF) models and SMOTE for Nominal and Continuous features (SMOTE-NC) oversam<sub>p</sub>lin<sub>g</sub>.

## 5.2 Evaluation Methodology

In current al<sub>g</sub>orithmic fairness literature<sub>,</sub> standard methodolo<sub>g</sub>ies and metrics <sub>p</sub>rimaril<sub>y</sub> tar<sub>g</sub>et binar<sub>y</sub> classification<sub>,</sub> fre<sub>q</sub>uentl<sub>y</sub> avoidin<sub>g</sub> an<sub>y</sub> multi-class discussion. To overcome these limitation<sub>,</sub> <sub>p</sub>ractitioners mi<sub>g</sub>ht resort to two main One-vs-Rest reduction a<sub>pp</sub>roaches to decom<sub>p</sub>ose a fair <sub>mu</sub>lti<sub>-c</sub>l<sub>ass</sub> <sub>c</sub>l<sub>ass</sub>ifi<sub>ca</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> t<sub>as</sub>k i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>o</sub> <sub>a</sub> <sub>se</sub>t <sub>o</sub>f bi<sub>nary</sub> <sub>c</sub>l<sub>ass</sub>ifi<sub>ca</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> <sub>su</sub>b<sub>-pro</sub>bl<sub>ems,</sub> di<sub>scusse</sub>d b<sub>e</sub>l<sub>ow.</sub>

The first a<sub>pp</sub>roach is called Tar<sub>g</sub>et Binarization and <sub>p</sub>artitions the label set Y into two subsets, ma<sub>pp</sub>in<sub>g</sub> the ori<sub>g</sub>inal multi-class tar<sub>g</sub>et $K = | \boldsymbol { y } |$ into a sin<sub>g</sub>le s<sub>y</sub>nthetic binar<sub>y</sub> tar<sub>g</sub>et. Given the new binar<sub>y</sub> tar<sub>g</sub>et<sub>,</sub> an<sub>y</sub> <sub>p</sub>referred o<sub>p</sub>timization strate<sub>gy</sub> can be used to train a binar<sub>y</sub> classifier<sub>,</sub> so that the model’s fairness can be evaluated usin<sub>g</sub> an<sub>y</sub> existin<sub>g</sub> binar<sub>y</sub> metric. This a<sub>pp</sub>roach has multiple limitations: (i) forced binarization of multi-class labels discards a lot of information; (ii) <sub>c</sub>l<sub>ass</sub> <sub>aggrega</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> d<sub>rama</sub>ti<sub>ca</sub>ll<sub>y</sub> <sub>a</sub>lt<sub>ers</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>c</sub>l<sub>ass</sub>ifi<sub>ca</sub>ti<sub>on</sub> t<sub>as</sub>k <sub>an</sub>d di<sub>s</sub>t<sub>or</sub>t<sub>s</sub> th<sub>e</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>seman</sub>ti<sub>c</sub> <sub>mean</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> of each label, which (iii) potentially exacerbates representation imbalances within disadvantaged protected groups, hence (iv) masks hidden disparities which could harm specific under-represented subgroups; ultimately (v) selecting an appropriate binary partition is dificult when multiple classes <sub>c</sub>h<sub>arac</sub>t<sub>er</sub>i<sub>ze</sub> hi<sub>g</sub>h<sub>-s</sub>t<sub>a</sub>k<sub>e scenar</sub>i<sub>os.</sub>

The second a<sub>pp</sub>roach is called Prediction Binarization trains a m<sub>u</sub>lti-class classifier on the ori<sub>g</sub>inal multi-class dataset<sub>,</sub> and then a<sub>pp</sub>lies <sub>p</sub>rediction binarization onl<sub>y</sub> durin<sub>g</sub> fairness evaluation. ## A Examples

In this Section we <sub>p</sub>rovide various illustrative scenarios which hi<sub>g</sub>hli<sub>g</sub>hts the diferences and desiderate a<sub>pp</sub>lications of each fairness criteria and fairness metric.

Example 1 (Fairness criteria diference: loan approval). A bank uses a ML model to approve or deny credit loans to their clients and that gender is selected as the sensitive attribute, considering, for the sake of simplicity, two groups: males and females.

Independence ensures the approval rates to be equal across each demographic group, e.g. males and females. Thus the applicant’s gender does not influence the approval rate.

Separation requires that correct classification rates and error rates must be equal for both males and females, thus removing any performance disparities between protected groups. Hence, any correlation between the prediction and the client’s gender is explained solely by applicant’s true creditworthiness.

Lastly, Suficiency impose that among clients whose loans are approved, the proportion who actually repay their loan is the same for every protected group, e.g males and females. Thus, once the decision is known, the applicant’s gender does not provide any information about whether they will repay the loan.

Example 2 (Binary Demographic Parity: Loan Allocation). Referring to Example 1, binary Demographic Parity requires that the ratio of loans granted $( { \hat { Y } } = 1 )$ to males is exactly equal to the ratio granted to females applicants:

$$
P ( \hat { Y } = 1 \mid A = M a l e ) = P ( \hat { Y } = 1 \mid A = F e m a l e ) .
$$

Because $P ( \hat { Y } = 0 \mid A = a ) = 1 - P ( \hat { Y } = 1 \mid A = a )$ , equalizing positive selection rates automatically guarantees equal denial rates $( P ( \hat { Y } = 0 \mid A = M a l e ) = P ( \hat { Y } = 0 \mid A = F e m a l e ) )$ . This ensures that applicant gender has no statistical influence on loan approval, irrespective ofunderlying historical repayment rates (�).

Example 3 (Multi-class and Multi-group Demographic Parity: Loan Allocation). Extending Example 1 to a multi-class and multi-group setting, suppose the bank ofers three distinct credit tiers: $\hat { Y } \in \lbrace 1 : M i c r o { - } L o a n , 2 \rbrace$ : Standard Credit, 3 : Premium Line}, evaluated across three demographic groups $A \in \{ a _ { 1 } , a _ { 2 } , a _ { 3 } \}$

Multi-class and Multi-group Demographic Parity mandates that the selection rate for every loan tier $\hat { y } \in \{ 1 , 2 , 3 \}$ must remain identical across all demographic groups:

$$
P ( \hat { Y } = \hat { y } \mid A = a _ { 1 } ) = P ( \hat { Y } = \hat { y } \mid A = a _ { 2 } ) = P ( \hat { Y } = \hat { y } \mid A = a _ { 3 } ) \quad \forall \hat { y } \in \{ 1 , 2 , 3 \} .
$$

If 15% of applicants in group �<sub>1</sub> are granted a Premium Line $( { \hat { Y } } = 3 ) ,$ exactly 15% of applicants from $a _ { 2 }$ and $a _ { 3 }$ must also receive a Premium Line. This prevents demographic concentration in lower credit tiers or systemic favouritism in higher tiers.

Example 4 (Binary Conditional Demographic Parity: Loan Approval). Revisiting Example 1, suppose credit score band $R \in \{ L o w ,$ Medium, High} is selected as a legitimate control variable (strata). Binary Conditional Demographic Parity requires that within each credit score stratum �, loan approval rates $( { \hat { Y } } = 1 )$ must be identical across protected groups:

$$
P ( { \hat { Y } } = 1 \mid A = M a l e , R = r ) = P ( { \hat { Y } } = 1 \mid A = F e m a l e , R = r ) \quad \forall r \in \{ L o w , M e d i u m , H i g h \} .
$$

While overall Demographic Parity might be violated if baseline credit scores difer across groups, Conditional Demographic Parity ensures that male and female applicants with identical credit scores receive equal approval probabilities.

Example 5 (Multi-class and Multi-Group Conditional Demographic Parity: Credit Allocation). Combining both generalizations for Example 1, consider the bank assigning applicants to three loan tiers $\hat { Y } \in \{ 1 , 2 , 3 \}$ across three demographic groups $A \in \{ a _ { 1 } , a _ { 2 } , a _ { 3 } \}$ , conditioned on income bracket strata $R \in \{ L o w \mathrm { - }$ Income, Middle-Income, High-Income}.

Multi-class Conditional Demographic Parity requires that for every income stratum $r \in { \mathcal { R } }$ and every credit tier $\hat { y } \in \{ 1 , 2 , 3 \}$ , selection rates are equal across all demographic groups:

$$
\begin{array} { r l r } { P ( \hat { Y } = \hat { y } \mid A = a _ { 1 } , R = r ) = P ( \hat { Y } = \hat { y } \mid A = a _ { 2 } , R = r ) } \\ { = P ( \hat { Y } = \hat { y } \mid A = a _ { 3 } , R = r ) , } & { } & { \forall \hat { y } \in \{ 1 , 2 , 3 \} , } \\ { \forall r \in \mathscr { R } . } \end{array}
$$

This formulation ensures that multi-tier financial products are distributed equitably across intersectional groups, controllingfor legitimate economic indicators (�) while eliminating residual demographic bias.

Example 6 (Equalized Odds vs. Equal Accuracy: Repaying a Loan). To illustrate this distinction, consider a toy dataset comprising four individuals: two males and two females, where exactly one male and one female are capable of repaying a loan $( Y = 1 )$ . Requiring equal confusion matrices implies that the model must commit the exact same types of misclassification with identical probabilities for each for both groups, deceptively suggesting a fair predictor. Conversely, evaluating the classifier under Equalized Odds exposes its severe discrimination against females.

sensitive group. Suppose a biased classifier consistently predicts loan approval for all males $( { \hat { Y } } = 1 )$ while denying all females $( { \hat { Y } } = 0 )$ . The resulting confusion matrices for each demographic group are presented in Table 4. Evaluating the model strictly through accuracy yields an identical score of50%
<table><tr><td colspan="3">Males</td><td colspan="3">Females</td></tr><tr><td></td><td> $Y = 0$ </td><td> $Y = 1$ </td><td></td><td> $Y = 0$ </td><td> $Y = 1$ </td></tr><tr><td> $\hat { Y } = 0$ </td><td>0</td><td>0</td><td> $\hat { Y } = 0$ </td><td>1</td><td>1</td></tr><tr><td> $\hat { Y } = 1$ </td><td>1</td><td></td><td> $\hat { Y } = 1$ </td><td>0</td><td>0</td></tr></table>

Table 4. Group-specific confusion matrices demonstrating identical accuracy (50%) despite severe error rate disparities.

Example 7 (Equal Opportunity: Disease detection). Disease detection is a representative application where Equal Opportunity is an appropriate fairness metric. Consider a predictive model deployed to detect a severe medical condition, where $Y = 1$ denotes the actual presence ofthe disease and $\hat { Y } = 1$ indicates a positive diagnosis. In this high-stakes context, the primary ethical imperative is ensuring that truly sick individuals $( Y = 1 )$ have an equal probability of receiving a correct diagnosis $\hat { \cal Y } = 1 )$ regardless of their demographic group �.

Ifa model fails to satisfy Equal Opportunity – for instance, by achieving a 90% True Positive Rate (TPR) for demographic � but only 60% for demographic � – it implicitly creates a severe disparity in False Negative Rates (FNR). Consequently, sick individuals in group � would face a significantly higher risk (40% vs. 10%) of going undiagnosed and untreated, leading to disparate healthcare outcomes despite having identical medical needs.

Example 8 (Predictive Equality: Criminal risk assessment). Criminal risk assessment provides a classic scenario where Predictive Equality is a desirable fairness metric. Consider a predictive model used by courts to assess recidivism risk, where $Y = 1$ denotes that an individual will reofend and $Y = 0$ indicates they will not, while $\hat { Y } = 1$ classifies an individual as high risk (potentially leading to bail denial or longer pre-trial detention). In this punitive context, a central ethical concern is avoiding disproportionate false accusations against individuals who would not reofend $( Y = 0 )$

Predictive Equality mandates that non-reofending individuals $( Y = 0 )$ face an equal probability of being falsely flagged as high risk $( { \hat { Y } } = 1 )$ , regardless of their demographic group �. If a model violates this criterion – for instance, exhibiting a 10% False Positive Rate (FPR) for group � but 30% for group $B - i t$ imposes a disproportionate penalty on low-risk individuals in group �. Consequently, non-reofending individuals in group � would face three times the risk of unjust detention, despite having an identical actual outcome of non-recidivism $( Y = 0 )$

Example 9 (Diagonal Equality: Multi-class Triage and Multi-Group Medical Assessment). Consider an automated hospital triage system classifying patient urgency into three distinct classes: $Y \in$ {1 : Routine, 2 : Urgent, 3 : Emergency}, deployed across a multi-group demographic population $A \in \{ a _ { 1 } , a _ { 2 } , a _ { 3 } \}$

Diagonal Equality restricts evaluation strictly to the diagonal entries of each group’s confusion matrix $( n _ { k k } ) .$ . It requires that patients who are genuinely in an Emergency state $( Y = 3 )$ have an identical detection rate across all demographic groups:

$$
P ( { \hat { Y } } = 3 \mid A = a _ { 1 } , Y = 3 ) = P ( { \hat { Y } } = 3 \mid A = a _ { 2 } , Y = 3 ) = P ( { \hat { Y } } = 3 \mid A = a _ { 3 } , Y = 3 ) .
$$

Simultaneously, the condition must hold independently for � = 2 (Urgent) and � = 1 (Routine).

Suppose the classifier achieves 90% recall for Class 3 among Group $^ { a _ { 1 } , }$ but only 60% for Group �<sub>3</sub>. In that case, critically ill patients in Group �<sub>3</sub> sufer a disproportionate risk ofmissing immediate emergency care. Notice, however, that this metric ignores how the remaining 40% of misclassified Group $a _ { 3 }$ patients are distributed across Class 1 or Class $\mathcal { Z } ;$ it strictly demands equal true positive rates for each true label state across all demographic slices.

Example 10 (Of-diagonal Equality: Multi-Tier Credit Allocation and Directional Misclassification). Consider an automated credit evaluation model assigning loan applicant risk profiles into three tiers: � ∈ {1 : Prime, 2 : Subprime, 3 : High Risk / Reject}, evaluated over three intersectional demographic groups $A \in \{ a _ { 1 } , a _ { 2 } , a _ { 3 } \}$

While Diagonal Equality regulates true positive decisions on the diagonal, Of-diagonal Equality evaluates all of-diagonal entries $( j \neq k )$ . This ensures that when errors occur, their specific directional distribution does not penalize any protected group.

For instance, consider true Subprime applicants $( Y = 2 )$ . A misclassification could either upgrade them to Prime $( { \hat { Y } } = 1 )$ or downgrade them to Reject $\hat { ( Y } = 3 )$ . Of-diagonal Equality requires that:

$$
P ( { \hat { Y } } = 3 \mid A = a _ { 1 } , Y = 2 ) = P ( { \hat { Y } } = 3 \mid A = a _ { 2 } , Y = 2 ) = P ( { \hat { Y } } = 3 \mid A = a _ { 3 } , Y = 2 ) .
$$

If 15% of true Subprime applicants in Group $a _ { 1 }$ are mistakenly rejected $( { \hat { Y } } = 3 )$ , but 45% ofSubprime applicants in Group $a _ { 3 }$ are rejected, the system exhibits severe misclassification bias against Group $a _ { 3 }$ By constraining all of-diagonal cells $n _ { k j } \ ( k \neq j )$ to be equal across $a _ { 1 } , a _ { 2 } ,$ and $a _ { 3 } .$ , Of-diagonal Parity guarantees that no demographic group is systematically pushed into worse error buckets.

Example 11 (Model Reliability vs. Sensitivity). To illustrate this distinction, consider an ML model developed to identify diseases, where � = 0 denotes a healthy patient and � = 1 denotes a diseased patient. Suppose a classifier correctly identifies all 90 healthy patients but detects only 1 out of 10 diseased patients (Table 5).

<table><tr><td rowspan="2">Predicted outcome  $\hat { Y }$ </td><td colspan="2">Actual outcome Y</td></tr><tr><td>Healthy (Y = 0) Diseased (Y = 1)</td><td></td></tr><tr><td> $H e a l t h y \hat { Y } = 0 )$ </td><td>90 (TN)</td><td>9 (FN)</td></tr><tr><td> $D i s e a s e d ( \hat { Y } = 1 )$ </td><td>0 (FP)</td><td>1 (TP)</td></tr></table>

Table 5. Confusion matrix illustrating the distinction between precision and recall: the classifier achieves perfect precision (100%) but detects only 10% of diseased patients.

Although the classifier performs poorly at capturing diseased patients – achieving a True Positive Rate (recall) of only 10% (equivalently, a False Negative Rate of 90%) – it attains a Precision of 100%. In other words, whenever the model predicts that a patient is diseased $( { \hat { Y } } = 1 ) $ , the prediction is always correct. Therefore, the model provides highly reliable positive predictions despite its limited sensitivity.

The previous example ignores protected groups for simplicity. Now consider a multi-group scenario where gender is the sensitive attribute � ∈ {Male, Female}. Ifthe classifier achieves a Precision of 100% for male patients but only 70% for female patients, a positive prediction carries diferent levels of reliability depending on the patient’s gender. Such a classifier violates Suficiency, since the predictive value of the same decision difers across demographic groups.

Example 12 (Binary Predictive Parity: Automated Hiring). Consider an automated resume screening model where $\hat { Y } = 1$ indicates an invitation to an interview and � = 1 indicates an applicant who would actually succeed in the role. Binary Predictive Parity demands that the PPV be equal across protected groups. If 80% of group � candidates invited to an interview $( { \hat { Y } } = 1 )$ turn out to be qualified $( Y = 1 )$ , but only 50% of group � candidates invited are qualified, the interview invitation carries unequal predictive validity. Recruiter confidence in positive decisions would vary by demographic group, introducing secondary bias into downstream human evaluations.

Example 13 (Multi-group Multi-class Predictive Parity: Multi-Tier Credit Allocation and Directional Misclassification). Consider an automated credit evaluation model assigning loan applicant risk profiles into three tiers: $Y \in \{ 1$ : Prime, 2 : Subprime, 3 : High Risk / Reject}, evaluated over three intersectional demographic groups $A \in \{ a _ { 1 } , a _ { 2 } , a _ { 3 } \}$

While Diagonal Equality regulates true positive decisions on the diagonal, Of-diagonal Equality evaluates all of-diagonal entries $( j \neq k )$ . This ensures that when errors occur, their specific directional distribution does not penalize any protected group.

For instance, consider true Subprime applicants $( Y = 2 )$ . A misclassification could either upgrade them to Prime $( { \hat { Y } } = 1 )$ or downgrade them to Reject $\hat { ( Y } = 3 )$ . Of-diagonal Equality requires that:

$$
P ( { \hat { Y } } = 3 \mid A = a _ { 1 } , Y = 2 ) = P ( { \hat { Y } } = 3 \mid A = a _ { 2 } , Y = 2 ) = P ( { \hat { Y } } = 3 \mid A = a _ { 3 } , Y = 2 ) .
$$

If 15% of true Subprime applicants in Group $a _ { 1 }$ are mistakenly rejected $( { \hat { Y } } = 3 )$ , but 45% ofSubprime applicants in Group $a _ { 3 }$ are rejected, the system exhibits severe misclassification bias against Group $a _ { 3 }$ By constraining all of-diagonal cells $n _ { k j } \ ( k \neq j )$ to be equal across $a _ { 1 } , a _ { 2 }$ , and $a _ { 3 } .$ , Of-diagonal Parity guarantees that no demographic group is systematically pushed into worse error buckets.

Example 14 (Equal Accuracy: Masking Disparate Error Types). Consider an automated diagnostic system evaluating medical urgency across three demographic groups $A \in \{ a _ { 1 } , a _ { 2 } , a _ { 3 } \}$ with target classes $Y \in \{ 1$ : Routine, 2 : Urgent, 3 : Emergency}.

Suppose the model achieves an identical overall accuracy of 85% across all three demographic groups:

$$
P ( { \hat { Y } } = Y \mid A = a _ { 1 } ) = P ( { \hat { Y } } = Y \mid A = a _ { 2 } ) = P ( { \hat { Y } } = Y \mid A = a _ { 3 } ) = 0 . 8 5 ,
$$

thereby perfectly satisfying Equal Accuracy. However, examining the underlying group confusion matrices reveals stark disparities in error distribution: for Group $a _ { 1 } { \mathrm { : } }$ , the 15% total error rate consists entirely of false positives on routine cases $( { \hat { Y } } ~ = ~ 2 ~ | ~ Y ~ = ~ 1 )$ , whereas for Group $a _ { 3 } .$ , the 15% total error rate consists entirely of dangerous false negatives on critical emergency cases $( { \hat { Y } } = 1 \mid Y = 3 )$ Although Equal Accuracy reports perfect demographic parity, Group $a _ { 3 }$ patients sufer life-threatening misdiagnoses, while Group $a _ { 1 }$ patients face minor administrative over-referrals. This demonstrates why Equal Accuracy is insuficient as a stand-alone fairness metric: by summing diagonal entries into a single scalar value, it obscures class-specific error distribution and directional harm across protected groups.

Let c be this common column vector. This means that for any true label $y \in \mathcal { Y }$ the conditional prediction vector is exactly c:

$$

Because our <sub>p</sub>remise ex<sub>p</sub>licitl<sub>y</sub> assumes $\hat { Y } \ne Y$ <sub>, we conc</sub>l<sub>u</sub>d<sub>e</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t th<sub>e ma</sub>t<sub>r</sub>i<sub>x canno</sub>t h<sub>ave a ran</sub>k <sub>o</sub>f 1<sub>.</sub> Th<sub>ere</sub>f<sub>ore,</sub> th<sub>e</sub> l<sub>ower</sub> b<sub>oun</sub>d <sub>mus</sub>t b<sub>e:</sub>

$$
\mathrm { R a n k } ( M ) \geq 2\tag{37}
$$

Conclude feasibility in the Multi-class framework. $$
2 \leq \mathrm { R a n k } ( M ) \leq k - 1\tag{38}
$$

Because we are o<sub>p</sub>eratin<sub>g</sub> strictl<sub>y</sub> in a multi-class settin<sub>g</sub> where $k > 2 ,$ <sub>,</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>upper</sub> b<sub>oun</sub>d $k - 1$ is at l<sub>eas</sub>t 2<sub>.</sub> C<sub>onsequen</sub>tl<sub>y,</sub> th<sub>e</sub> i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>erva</sub>l $\left[ 2 , k - 1 \right]$ is a <sub>v</sub>alid<sub>,</sub> non-em<sub>p</sub>t<sub>y</sub> mathematical s<sub>p</sub>ace. This formall<sub>y</sub> <sub>proves</sub> th<sub>a</sub>t th<sub>ere ex</sub>i<sub>s</sub>t <sub>va</sub>lid <sub>ma</sub>t<sub>r</sub>i<sub>ces</sub> � <sub>o</sub>f <sub>ran</sub>k $r \geq 2$ (ensurin<sub>g p</sub>redictive <sub>p</sub>ower, $i . e . , \hat { Y } \not \in Y )$ th<sub>a</sub>t a<sup>l</sup>so possess a nu<sup>ll</sup> space capa<sup>bl</sup>e o<sup>f</sup> projecting t<sup>h</sup>e <sup>d</sup>emograp<sup>h</sup>ic <sup>d</sup>isparities $\Delta { \bf p } _ { a }$ t<sub>o</sub> <sub>zero.</sub> U<sub>n</sub>d<sub>er</sub> th<sub>ese</sub> s<sub>p</sub>ecific conditions<sub>,</sub> Inde<sub>p</sub>endence and Se<sub>p</sub>aration mutuall<sub>y</sub> coexist.

Proof 4 (Proof Mutual Exclusion of Separation and Suficiency). Apply Bayes’ Rule to the Suficiency Condition. By Bayes’ theorem, the joint probability $P ( Y = k \mid \hat { Y } = j , A = a )$ <sub>can</sub> b<sub>e expresse</sub>d <sub>as:</sub>

$$
P ( Y = k \mid \hat { Y } = j , A = a ) = \frac { P ( \hat { Y } = j \mid Y = k , A = a ) P ( Y = k \mid A = a ) } { P ( \hat { Y } = j \mid A = a ) }\tag{39}
$$

Substitute the Separation Assumption. Assuming Separation $( \hat { Y } \perp A \mid Y )$ h<sub>o</sub>ld<sub>s, we su</sub>b<sub>s</sub>tit<sub>u</sub>t<sub>e</sub> $P ( \hat { Y } = j \mid Y = k , A = a ) = P ( \hat { Y } = j \mid Y = k )$ i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>o</sub> th<sub>e numera</sub>t<sub>or:</sub>

$$
P ( Y = k \mid \hat { Y } = j , A = a ) = \frac { P ( \hat { Y } = j \mid Y = k ) P ( Y = k \mid A = a ) } { P ( \hat { Y } = j \mid A = a ) }\tag{40}
$$

Apply the Suficiency Assumption. Assuming Suficiency $( Y \perp A \mid \hat { Y } )$ <sub>a</sub>l<sub>so</sub> h<sub>o</sub>ld<sub>s,</sub> th<sub>e</sub> <sub>con</sub>diti<sub>ona</sub>l <sub>p</sub>osterior <sub>p</sub>robabilit<sub>y</sub> must e<sub>q</sub>ual the mar<sub>g</sub>inal <sub>p</sub>osterior <sub>p</sub>robabilit<sub>y</sub> $P ( Y = k \mid \hat { Y } = j )$

$$
P ( Y = k \mid { \hat { Y } } = j , A = a ) = P ( Y = k \mid { \hat { Y } } = j ) = { \frac { P ( { \hat { Y } } = j \mid Y = k ) P ( Y = k ) } { P ( { \hat { Y } } = j ) } }\tag{41}
$$

Equate Both Formulations. Equating the Expressions 40 and Expression 41 yields:

$$
\frac { P ( \hat { Y } = j \mid Y = k ) P ( Y = k \mid A = a ) } { P ( \hat { Y } = j \mid A = a ) } = \frac { P ( \hat { Y } = j \mid Y = k ) P ( Y = k ) } { P ( \hat { Y } = j ) }\tag{42}
$$

Cancel Likelihood Terms and Rearrange Ratios. Since $P ( \hat { Y } = j \mid Y = k ) > 0$ <sup>b</sup>y <sup>non-de</sup>g<sup>enerac</sup>y, <sub>we</sub> di<sub>v</sub>id<sub>e</sub> b<sub>o</sub>th <sub>s</sub>id<sub>es</sub> b<sub>y</sub> $P ( \hat { Y } = j \mid Y = k )$ :

$$
\frac { P ( Y = k \mid A = a ) } { P ( \hat { Y } = j \mid A = a ) } = \frac { P ( Y = k ) } { P ( \hat { Y } = j ) }\tag{43}
$$

Rearran<sub>g</sub>in<sub>g</sub> the terms to isolate the tar<sub>g</sub>et <sub>p</sub>robabilities on the left and <sub>p</sub>rediction <sub>p</sub>robabilities on the ri<sub>g</sub>ht <sub>g</sub>ives:

$$
{ \frac { P ( Y = k \mid A = a ) } { P ( Y = k ) } } = { \frac { P ( { \hat { Y } } = j \mid A = a ) } { P ( { \hat { Y } } = j ) } } \quad \forall k \in { \pmb { y } }\tag{44}
$$

Deduce Independence via Ratio Constancy. Notice that the left-hand side of the equation depends so<sup>l</sup>e<sup>l</sup>y on <sup>�</sup> an<sup>d</sup> �, w<sup>h</sup>ereas t<sup>h</sup>e rig<sup>h</sup>t-<sup>h</sup>an<sup>d</sup> si<sup>d</sup>e <sup>d</sup>epen<sup>d</sup>s so<sup>l</sup>e<sup>l</sup>y on � an<sup>d</sup> �. For t<sup>h</sup>is equa<sup>l</sup>ity to <sup>h</sup>o<sup>ld</sup> f<sub>or a</sub>ll $k \in \mathcal { Y }$ <sub>,</sub> th<sub>e ra</sub>ti<sub>o mus</sub>t b<sub>e equa</sub>l t<sub>o a group-</sub>d<sub>epen</sub>d<sub>en</sub>t <sub>cons</sub>t<sub>an</sub>t $C _ { a }$ th<sub>a</sub>t i<sub>s</sub> i<sub>n</sub>d<sub>epen</sub>d<sub>en</sub>t <sub>o</sub>f b<sub>o</sub>th � an<sup>d</sup> <sup>�</sup>:

$$
{ \frac { P ( Y = k \mid A = a ) } { P ( Y = k ) } } = C _ { a } \implies P ( Y = k \mid A = a ) = C _ { a } P ( Y = k ) \quad \forall k \in \mathcal { Y }\tag{45}
$$

S<sub>umm</sub>i<sub>ng</sub> b<sub>o</sub>th <sub>s</sub>id<sub>es over a</sub>ll <sub>poss</sub>ibl<sub>e</sub> t<sub>arge</sub>t <sub>c</sub>l<sub>asses</sub> $k \in \mathcal { Y }$

$$

Conclude Contradiction. Substituting $C _ { a } = 1$ b<sub>ac</sub>k i<sub>n</sub>t<sub>o</sub> th<sub>e ra</sub>ti<sub>o y</sub>i<sub>e</sub>ld<sub>s:</sub>

$$
